People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, um, it's just... I've been having a really hard time at work lately, and it's starting to become a real problem. I'm a hairstylist, you know, and usually, I'm pretty quick on my feet, getting through my appointments, chatting with clients, the whole thing. But recently, it's like... I don't know how to explain it, but my hands feel like they’re moving through glue. It's the weirdest sensation. Like, even just doing the simple things I've done a million times, like sectioning someone's hair before a cut or, or mixing up the color in the bowl—which usually takes me like two seconds—it just takes me forever now. It's like my brain is telling my hands to move, but they're just dragging. And the worst part is, it's not just me feeling it in my head. People have started noticing. My manager pulled me aside the other day and asked if I was feeling okay because I'm just so much slower than I used to be, and I didn't know what to tell her because I don't really know what's going on myself, just that everything feels so heavy and takes so much effort now.

But it takes so much energy to fake it. By the time I get home, I’m just completely drained, and that heavy sadness is still right there waiting for me. It’s like a dark cloud that just follows me from the minute I open my eyes until I finally manage to fall asleep. It’s exhausting, honestly. Just... exhausting.

Yeah, I... I really have. It’s like all the color has just drained out of everything. I used to love my job, I really did. I was always the one chatting up my clients, asking about their kids, their vacations, you know, just really connecting with them. I even used to stay late sometimes just to help the newer stylists practice their foiling techniques or color melting because I genuinely enjoyed it. I felt like I had this spark, this energy for it. I loved being outdoors, breathing the fresh air. 

But now... I don't know, it's just gone. That spark is completely gone. Now when I’m at work, I just keep my head down. I barely say a word to my clients unless I absolutely have to ask them about their hair. I don't want to talk, I don't want to engage. I just want to get through the cut and go hide in the breakroom. I avoid the other stylists, too. If I see them gathering to chat, I just walk the other way. And my friends... they keep texting me to go hiking or grab coffee, and I just make up excuses or ignore them. The thought of going out, of trying to pretend I care about a hike or a conversation... it just feels impossible. I don't get any joy out of it anymore. It’s like my battery is completely dead and I don't even care enough to try and recharge it. I just want to be left alone, which is so weird for me because I've always been such a people person, but now... I just don't have it in me.

I mean... yeah. It’s hard to say out loud, but yes. I want to be really clear though, I don’t have a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. I’m not sitting at home looking at my shears or trying to figure out how to do something drastic. I don't think I could ever actually do that. But... It’s just... it’s exhausting for me, but it has to be exhausting for them, too, dealing with someone who is just a total drag now. So yeah, I don't want to actively kill myself, but I just catch myself wishing that my body would just stop in my sleep. Just let me not wake up, so I don't have to keep forcing myself through every single day and dragging everyone else down with me. It just really feels like it would be a relief for everyone involved.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, um, it's just... I've been having a really hard time at work lately, and it's starting to become a real problem. I'm a hairstylist, you know, and usually, I'm pretty quick on my feet, getting through my appointments, chatting with clients, the whole thing. But recently, it's like... I don't know how to explain it, but my hands feel like they’re moving through glue. It's the weirdest sensation. Like, even just doing the simple things I've done a million times, like sectioning someone's hair before a cut or, or mixing up the color in the bowl—which usually takes me like two seconds—it just takes me forever now. It's like my brain is telling my hands to move, but they're just dragging. And the worst part is, it's not just me feeling it in my head. People have started noticing. My manager pulled me aside the other day and asked if I was feeling okay because I'm just so much slower than I used to be, and I didn't know what to tell her because I don't really know what's going on myself, just that everything feels so heavy and takes so much effort now.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have a tremor in your hands when they’re at rest?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any shaking or tremors when I'm just sitting there. Like, if I'm just sitting in the breakroom on my phone, or watching TV at home, my hands are completely still. It's really just that heavy, dragging feeling when I actually try to use them. Like I said, it's just so bizarre because I'm used to being so fast. I mean, you have to be fast in a salon, especially on a Saturday when you have back-to-back clients and someone's processing while you're cutting someone else. You're constantly moving, constantly using your hands. And now, it's just this incredible effort to even lift the hairdryer. But no, no shaking at all when I'm resting them. Just the slowness. It's so frustrating because my mind knows what to do, but my body just won't cooperate, you know? It's like I'm moving underwater. But yeah, no tremors.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed or down most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I have. It’s actually... well, it’s most days, honestly. Like, I’ll wake up, and before I even get out of bed, there’s just this... this heavy, flat sadness sitting right in the middle of my chest. It’s not even like a sharp pain or crying sadness, it’s just this dull, heavy weight that makes it hard to even breathe sometimes. And it’s just there, all day. It makes me feel like... like there’s just no point in trying to build the life I was planning, you know? \n\nI had all these plans when I got my associate's and started at the salon. I was going to build up my clientele, maybe open my own suite eventually... but now, with this feeling, it just seems so impossible and pointless. Why even try when everything feels this bleak? I try to push through it, especially at work, because you have to put on a happy face for the clients. You can't just stand behind the chair looking miserable while you're doing a balayage. But it takes so much energy to fake it. By the time I get home, I’m just completely drained, and that heavy sadness is still right there waiting for me. It’s like a dark cloud that just follows me from the minute I open my eyes until I finally manage to fall asleep. It’s exhausting, honestly. Just... exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I really have. It’s like all the color has just drained out of everything. I used to love my job, I really did. I was always the one chatting up my clients, asking about their kids, their vacations, you know, just really connecting with them. I even used to stay late sometimes just to help the newer stylists practice their foiling techniques or color melting because I genuinely enjoyed it. I felt like I had this spark, this energy for it. And on my days off, I was always out doing something. I’d go hiking with my friends up at the state park—we’d pack a lunch, spend the whole day out there, just laughing and taking pictures. I loved being outdoors, breathing the fresh air. \n\nBut now... I don't know, it's just gone. That spark is completely gone. Now when I’m at work, I just keep my head down. I barely say a word to my clients unless I absolutely have to ask them about their hair. I don't want to talk, I don't want to engage. I just want to get through the cut and go hide in the breakroom. I avoid the other stylists, too. If I see them gathering to chat, I just walk the other way. And my friends... they keep texting me to go hiking or grab coffee, and I just make up excuses or ignore them. The thought of going out, of trying to pretend I care about a hike or a conversation... it just feels impossible. I don't get any joy out of it anymore. It’s like my battery is completely dead and I don't even care enough to try and recharge it. I just want to be left alone, which is so weird for me because I've always been such a people person, but now... I just don't have it in me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living or that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I mean... yeah. It’s hard to say out loud, but yes. I want to be really clear though, I don’t have a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. I’m not sitting at home looking at my shears or trying to figure out how to do something drastic. I don't think I could ever actually do that. But... I do get these thoughts, especially at night when I’m just lying there in the dark after a long shift. It just feels like everything is so incredibly heavy, and I catch myself thinking... I just think it would be so much easier if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. You know? Just drifted off and that was the end of it. \n\nBecause then I wouldn't have to wake up and face that horrible, flat feeling in my chest again, and... and honestly, nobody would have to deal with me anymore. Because I really feel like I’m just becoming this huge burden to everyone around me. Like my friends, the ones I mentioned who keep texting me to go out? I know they’re getting frustrated with me constantly flaking on them. I can tell from the tone of their messages, they're just over it. And at the salon, my manager is definitely noticing that I’m acting weird and not talking to my clients or the other girls. It’s just... it’s exhausting for me, but it has to be exhausting for them, too, dealing with someone who is just a total drag now. So yeah, I don't want to actively kill myself, but I just catch myself wishing that my body would just stop in my sleep. Just let me not wake up, so I don't have to keep forcing myself through every single day and dragging everyone else down with me. It just really feels like it would be a relief for everyone involved.\n\nDoctor: Have you started, stopped, or changed any medication or substance use recently?\n\nPatient: No... no, I haven't. I mean, I don't really take anything to begin with, honestly. Like, I'm not on any prescriptions or anything like that. The most I ever take is maybe some ibuprofen or Advil, you know, because standing behind the chair for eight or ten hours a day, your lower back and your feet just start screaming at you by the end of the shift. Especially when you're doing a long color correction or a full head of babylights where you're just stuck in one spot and you can't really sit down or take a break. But other than that, no. I don't take any daily pills or anything. \n\nAnd as far as, like, other stuff... I don't really drink or do drugs. I mean, back when I was in school getting my associate's, or even when I first started at the salon, the other stylists and I would sometimes go out for margaritas on a Friday night after we closed up, just to blow off some steam and complain about difficult clients or whatever. But I haven't done that in... I don't even know how long. Like I said earlier, I just avoid everyone now, so I definitely haven't been going out drinking. And I've never been into anything else, no smoking or whatever. So, yeah, nothing has changed. I'm not putting anything new into my body, and I haven't stopped taking anything because there was nothing to stop in the first place. It's just me and this heavy feeling, really. Just dealing with it on my own without taking anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I... I don't think so. Unusually energetic? No, definitely not. Honestly, I can't even remember the last time I felt just normal energy, let alone extra energy. Like I said, it’s just this constant, heavy, dead-battery feeling all the time. I've never had a time where I didn't need to sleep. If anything, it's the total opposite right now. I just want to sleep all the time, even though I don't sleep well when I actually try. But no, I've never had a few days where I felt like I could conquer the world or anything like that. 

I mean, I used to be confident, sure. Like, when I first got my chair at the salon, I was so proud of myself. I felt like I really knew what I was doing, especially with color. I was always the one the manager would ask to double-check a toner formula or something because I just had a good eye for it. I felt really solid in my skills. But it wasn't, like, *overly* confident, you know? It was just normal confidence because I worked hard for my associate's and I practiced a lot. I never felt like I was invincible or better than everyone else or anything crazy like that. And I certainly never felt like I didn't need sleep. Sleep has always been really important to me, especially standing on my feet all day. If I don't get at least seven or eight hours, I'm just a wreck the next day at work. My hands get a little shaky when I'm trying to do precision cuts if I'm tired, so I've always prioritized sleep. So, no, I've never had anything like what you're describing. Never any bursts of crazy energy or not needing to sleep. Just the normal me, and now... whatever this version of me is.

No, never anything like that. I mean, my brain... well, besides all the weird stuff I've been telling you about with feeling so empty and the voices and everything... physically, my brain is fine. At least, I think it is. I've never had a doctor tell me otherwise. Like, I've never had a seizure or a stroke or anything scary like that. I don't have epilepsy. But that usually goes away if I just take a hot shower and kind of massage the back of my neck. 

I did hit my head once when I was little, maybe like seven or eight? I was at the park with my mom and I fell off the monkey bars and bumped my forehead on the woodchips. I got a pretty good goosebump, and my mom was so worried she took me to the pediatrician, but they just said to keep an eye on me and make sure I didn't throw up or fall asleep immediately. And I was totally fine, I was back to running around the next day. But that's literally the only time I can even think of where my head was involved in any kind of medical thing. Especially when you're doing a long color correction or a full head of babylights where you're just stuck in one spot and you can't really sit down or take a break. But other than that, no. I don't take any daily pills or anything. \n\nAnd as far as, like, other stuff... I don't really drink or do drugs. I mean, back when I was in school getting my associate's, or even when I first started at the salon, the other stylists and I would sometimes go out for margaritas on a Friday night after we closed up, just to blow off some steam and complain about difficult clients or whatever. But I haven't done that in... I don't even know how long. Like I said earlier, I just avoid everyone now, so I definitely haven't been going out drinking. And I've never been into anything else, no smoking or whatever. So, yeah, nothing has changed. I'm not putting anything new into my body, and I haven't stopped taking anything because there was nothing to stop in the first place. It's just me and this heavy feeling, really. Just dealing with it on my own without taking anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a few days when you felt unusually energetic or overly confident, with much less need for sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. Unusually energetic? No, definitely not. Honestly, I can't even remember the last time I felt just normal energy, let alone extra energy. Like I said, it’s just this constant, heavy, dead-battery feeling all the time. I've never had a time where I didn't need to sleep. If anything, it's the total opposite right now. I just want to sleep all the time, even though I don't sleep well when I actually try. But no, I've never had a few days where I felt like I could conquer the world or anything like that. \n\nI mean, I used to be confident, sure. Like, when I first got my chair at the salon, I was so proud of myself. I felt like I really knew what I was doing, especially with color. I was always the one the manager would ask to double-check a toner formula or something because I just had a good eye for it. I felt really solid in my skills. But it wasn't, like, *overly* confident, you know? It was just normal confidence because I worked hard for my associate's and I practiced a lot. I never felt like I was invincible or better than everyone else or anything crazy like that. And I certainly never felt like I didn't need sleep. Sleep has always been really important to me, especially standing on my feet all day. If I don't get at least seven or eight hours, I'm just a wreck the next day at work. My hands get a little shaky when I'm trying to do precision cuts if I'm tired, so I've always prioritized sleep. So, no, I've never had anything like what you're describing. Never any bursts of crazy energy or not needing to sleep. Just the normal me, and now... whatever this version of me is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with, or treated for, any brain or neurological condition?\n\nPatient: No, never anything like that. I mean, my brain... well, besides all the weird stuff I've been telling you about with feeling so empty and the voices and everything... physically, my brain is fine. At least, I think it is. I've never had a doctor tell me otherwise. Like, I've never had a seizure or a stroke or anything scary like that. I don't have epilepsy. I don't even really get bad headaches, to be honest. Sometimes I get a little bit of a tension headache right at the base of my neck, but that's just from looking down at people's hair all day, you know? When you're constantly angling your head down to check a guideline or make sure a bob is perfectly straight, your neck just gets so stiff, and that can kind of travel up into your head. But that usually goes away if I just take a hot shower and kind of massage the back of my neck. \n\nI did hit my head once when I was little, maybe like seven or eight? I was at the park with my mom and I fell off the monkey bars and bumped my forehead on the woodchips. I got a pretty good goosebump, and my mom was so worried she took me to the pediatrician, but they just said to keep an eye on me and make sure I didn't throw up or fall asleep immediately. And I was totally fine, I was back to running around the next day. But that's literally the only time I can even think of where my head was involved in any kind of medical thing. I've never had an MRI or a CT scan or anything where they actually looked at my brain. So, as far as I know, neurologically, everything is completely normal. It's just... whatever is happening to me now, it doesn't feel like a physical brain problem, you know? It feels different. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah... actually, yes. It’s... it’s kind of hard to talk about because it sounds so crazy, but yes. I actually mentioned voices a minute ago, and this is exactly what I meant. It happens at the salon mostly. 

You know how at the end of the day, or even just between clients if you have a minute, you have to do all this cleanup? Like sweeping up the hair around the chairs—which gets absolutely everywhere, by the way, it sticks to your shoes and your clothes—or taking all the dirty color bowls and brushes to the back sink to rinse them out before the color dries and stains the plastic? Well, sometimes when I’m doing that... like when I’m just quietly sweeping up or standing there rinsing out the bowls... I hear this voice. 

It’s a woman’s voice. And she sounds like she’s right over by the shampoo stations. Our shampoo bowls are kind of tucked into this little alcove near the back of the floor, so it echoes just a tiny bit, but I can hear her so clearly. She just keeps saying that I’m ruining everything. Just... "you're ruining everything." And her tone is so cruel. 

The first time it happened, I actually dropped the broom and walked over to the bowls because I thought maybe one of the other stylists was back there on the phone having a fight with someone, or maybe a client had wandered back looking for the restroom and was upset. But there was nobody there. And it keeps happening even when the salon is completely empty. Like, if I’m the closing stylist for the night and I’ve already locked the front door, flipped the sign, and turned off the main lights, and it’s literally just me in the building finishing up... I’ll still hear her. Right near the shampoo stations. Just telling me I’m ruining everything. 

It makes me freeze up, honestly. I just stand there holding a wet tint brush or whatever, and my heart starts pounding because I know for a fact I’m the only one in the building, or that all the other girls are up at the front desk, but the voice is just so real. It's right there. I haven't really *seen* anything, though. No weird shadows or people who aren't there, nothing like that. It's really just hearing her. And it's always when I'm trying to clean up, and she just won't stop telling me how I'm ruining it all.

I don't know how to explain it, but my hands feel like they’re moving through glue. It's the weirdest sensation. Like, even just doing the simple things I've done a million times, like sectioning someone's hair before a cut or, or mixing up the color in the bowl—which usually takes me like two seconds—it just takes me forever now. It's like my brain is telling my hands to move, but they're just dragging. And the worst part is, it's not just me feeling it in my head. People have started noticing. My manager pulled me aside the other day and asked if I was feeling okay because I'm just so much slower than I used to be, and I didn't know what to tell her because I don't really know what's going on myself, just that everything feels so heavy and takes so much effort now.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have a tremor in your hands when they’re at rest?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any shaking or tremors when I'm just sitting there. Like, if I'm just sitting in the breakroom on my phone, or watching TV at home, my hands are completely still. It's really just that heavy, dragging feeling when I actually try to use them. Like I said, it's just so bizarre because I'm used to being so fast. I mean, you have to be fast in a salon, especially on a Saturday when you have back-to-back clients and someone's processing while you're cutting someone else. You're constantly moving, constantly using your hands. And now, it's just this incredible effort to even lift the hairdryer. But no, no shaking at all when I'm resting them. Just the slowness. It's so frustrating because my mind knows what to do, but my body just won't cooperate, you know? It's like I'm moving underwater. But yeah, no tremors.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed or down most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I have. It’s actually... well, it’s most days, honestly. Like, I’ll wake up, and before I even get out of bed, there’s just this... this heavy, flat sadness sitting right in the middle of my chest. It’s not even like a sharp pain or crying sadness, it’s just this dull, heavy weight that makes it hard to even breathe sometimes. And it’s just there, all day. It makes me feel like... like there’s just no point in trying to build the life I was planning, you know? \n\nI had all these plans when I got my associate's and started at the salon. I was going to build up my clientele, maybe open my own suite eventually... but now, with this feeling, it just seems so impossible and pointless. Why even try when everything feels this bleak? I try to push through it, especially at work, because you have to put on a happy face for the clients. You can't just stand behind the chair looking miserable while you're doing a balayage. But it takes so much energy to fake it. By the time I get home, I’m just completely drained, and that heavy sadness is still right there waiting for me. It’s like a dark cloud that just follows me from the minute I open my eyes until I finally manage to fall asleep. It’s exhausting, honestly. Just... exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I really have. It’s like all the color has just drained out of everything. I even used to stay late sometimes just to help the newer stylists practice their foiling techniques or color melting because I genuinely enjoyed it. I felt like I had this spark, this energy for it. And on my days off, I was always out doing something. I’d go hiking with my friends up at the state park—we’d pack a lunch, spend the whole day out there, just laughing and taking pictures. I loved being outdoors, breathing the fresh air. \n\nBut now... I don't know, it's just gone. That spark is completely gone. Now when I’m at work, I just keep my head down. I barely say a word to my clients unless I absolutely have to ask them about their hair. I don't want to talk, I don't want to engage. I just want to get through the cut and go hide in the breakroom. I avoid the other stylists, too. If I see them gathering to chat, I just walk the other way. And my friends... they keep texting me to go hiking or grab coffee, and I just make up excuses or ignore them. The thought of going out, of trying to pretend I care about a hike or a conversation... it just feels impossible. I don't get any joy out of it anymore. It’s like my battery is completely dead and I don't even care enough to try and recharge it. I just want to be left alone, which is so weird for me because I've always been such a people person, but now... I just don't have it in me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living or that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I mean... yeah. It’s hard to say out loud, but yes. I want to be really clear though, I don’t have a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. I’m not sitting at home looking at my shears or trying to figure out how to do something drastic. I don't think I could ever actually do that. But... I do get these thoughts, especially at night when I’m just lying there in the dark after a long shift. It just feels like everything is so incredibly heavy, and I catch myself thinking... I just think it would be so much easier if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. You know? Just drifted off and that was the end of it. \n\nBecause then I wouldn't have to wake up and face that horrible, flat feeling in my chest again, and... and honestly, nobody would have to deal with me anymore. The thought of going out, of trying to pretend I care about a hike or a conversation... it just feels impossible. I don't get any joy out of it anymore. It’s like my battery is completely dead and I don't even care enough to try and recharge it. I just want to be left alone, which is so weird for me because I've always been such a people person, but now... I just don't have it in me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living or that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I mean... yeah. It’s hard to say out loud, but yes. I want to be really clear though, I don’t have a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. I’m not sitting at home looking at my shears or trying to figure out how to do something drastic. I don't think I could ever actually do that. But... I do get these thoughts, especially at night when I’m just lying there in the dark after a long shift. It just feels like everything is so incredibly heavy, and I catch myself thinking... I just think it would be so much easier if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. You know? Just drifted off and that was the end of it. \n\nBecause then I wouldn't have to wake up and face that horrible, flat feeling in my chest again, and... and honestly, nobody would have to deal with me anymore. Because I really feel like I’m just becoming this huge burden to everyone around me. Like my friends, the ones I mentioned who keep texting me to go out? I know they’re getting frustrated with me constantly flaking on them. I can tell from the tone of their messages, they're just over it. And at the salon, my manager is definitely noticing that I’m acting weird and not talking to my clients or the other girls. It’s just... it’s exhausting for me, but it has to be exhausting for them, too, dealing with someone who is just a total drag now. So yeah, I don't want to actively kill myself, but I just catch myself wishing that my body would just stop in my sleep. Just let me not wake up, so I don't have to keep forcing myself through every single day and dragging everyone else down with me. It just really feels like it would be a relief for everyone involved.\n\nDoctor: Have you started, stopped, or changed any medication or substance use recently?\n\nPatient: No... no, I haven't. I mean, I don't really take anything to begin with, honestly. Like, I'm not on any prescriptions or anything like that. The most I ever take is maybe some ibuprofen or Advil, you know, because standing behind the chair for eight or ten hours a day, your lower back and your feet just start screaming at you by the end of the shift. Especially when you're doing a long color correction or a full head of babylights where you're just stuck in one spot and you can't really sit down or take a break. But other than that, no. I don't take any daily pills or anything. \n\nAnd as far as, like, other stuff... I don't really drink or do drugs. I mean, back when I was in school getting my associate's, or even when I first started at the salon, the other stylists and I would sometimes go out for margaritas on a Friday night after we closed up, just to blow off some steam and complain about difficult clients or whatever. But I haven't done that in... I don't even know how long. Like I said earlier, I just avoid everyone now, so I definitely haven't been going out drinking. And I've never been into anything else, no smoking or whatever. So, yeah, nothing has changed. I'm not putting anything new into my body, and I haven't stopped taking anything because there was nothing to stop in the first place. It's just me and this heavy feeling, really. Just dealing with it on my own without taking anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a few days when you felt unusually energetic or overly confident, with much less need for sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. Unusually energetic? No, definitely not. Honestly, I can't even remember the last time I felt just normal energy, let alone extra energy. Like I said, it’s just this constant, heavy, dead-battery feeling all the time. I've never had a time where I didn't need to sleep. If anything, it's the total opposite right now. I just want to sleep all the time, even though I don't sleep well when I actually try. But no, I've never had a few days where I felt like I could conquer the world or anything like that. \n\nI mean, I used to be confident, sure. Like, when I first got my chair at the salon, I was so proud of myself. I felt like I really knew what I was doing, especially with color. I was always the one the manager would ask to double-check a toner formula or something because I just had a good eye for it. I felt really solid in my skills. But it wasn't, like, *overly* confident, you know? It was just normal confidence because I worked hard for my associate's and I practiced a lot. I never felt like I was invincible or better than everyone else or anything crazy like that. And I certainly never felt like I didn't need sleep. Sleep has always been really important to me, especially standing on my feet all day. If I don't get at least seven or eight hours, I'm just a wreck the next day at work. My hands get a little shaky when I'm trying to do precision cuts if I'm tired, so I've always prioritized sleep. So, no, I've never had anything like what you're describing. Never any bursts of crazy energy or not needing to sleep. Just the normal me, and now... whatever this version of me is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with, or treated for, any brain or neurological condition?\n\nPatient: No, never anything like that. I mean, my brain... well, besides all the weird stuff I've been telling you about with feeling so empty and the voices and everything... physically, my brain is fine. At least, I think it is. I've never had a doctor tell me otherwise. Like, I've never had a seizure or a stroke or anything scary like that. I don't have epilepsy. I don't even really get bad headaches, to be honest. Sometimes I get a little bit of a tension headache right at the base of my neck, but that's just from looking down at people's hair all day, you know? When you're constantly angling your head down to check a guideline or make sure a bob is perfectly straight, your neck just gets so stiff, and that can kind of travel up into your head. But that usually goes away if I just take a hot shower and kind of massage the back of my neck. \n\nI did hit my head once when I was little, maybe like seven or eight? I was at the park with my mom and I fell off the monkey bars and bumped my forehead on the woodchips. I got a pretty good goosebump, and my mom was so worried she took me to the pediatrician, but they just said to keep an eye on me and make sure I didn't throw up or fall asleep immediately. And I was totally fine, I was back to running around the next day. But that's literally the only time I can even think of where my head was involved in any kind of medical thing. I've never had an MRI or a CT scan or anything where they actually looked at my brain. So, as far as I know, neurologically, everything is completely normal. It's just... whatever is happening to me now, it doesn't feel like a physical brain problem, you know? It feels different. But yeah, no official diagnoses or treatments for anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don’t seem to hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yeah... actually, yes. It’s... it’s kind of hard to talk about because it sounds so crazy, but yes. I actually mentioned voices a minute ago, and this is exactly what I meant. It happens at the salon mostly. \n\nYou know how at the end of the day, or even just between clients if you have a minute, you have to do all this cleanup? Like sweeping up the hair around the chairs—which gets absolutely everywhere, by the way, it sticks to your shoes and your clothes—or taking all the dirty color bowls and brushes to the back sink to rinse them out before the color dries and stains the plastic? Well, sometimes when I’m doing that... like when I’m just quietly sweeping up or standing there rinsing out the bowls... I hear this voice. \n\nIt’s a woman’s voice. And she sounds like she’s right over by the shampoo stations. Our shampoo bowls are kind of tucked into this little alcove near the back of the floor, so it echoes just a tiny bit, but I can hear her so clearly. She just keeps saying that I’m ruining everything. Just... \"you're ruining everything.\" And her tone is so cruel. \n\nThe first time it happened, I actually dropped the broom and walked over to the bowls because I thought maybe one of the other stylists was back there on the phone having a fight with someone, or maybe a client had wandered back looking for the restroom and was upset. But there was nobody there. And it keeps happening even when the salon is completely empty. Like, if I’m the closing stylist for the night and I’ve already locked the front door, flipped the sign, and turned off the main lights, and it’s literally just me in the building finishing up... I’ll still hear her. Right near the shampoo stations. Just telling me I’m ruining everything. \n\nIt makes me freeze up, honestly. I just stand there holding a wet tint brush or whatever, and my heart starts pounding because I know for a fact I’m the only one in the building, or that all the other girls are up at the front desk, but the voice is just so real. It's right there. I haven't really *seen* anything, though. No weird shadows or people who aren't there, nothing like that. It's really just hearing her. And it's always when I'm trying to clean up, and she just won't stop telling me how I'm ruining it all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

And it's always when I'm trying to clean up, and she just won't stop telling me how I'm ruining it all.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo the voices or other unusual experiences happen only when your mood is at its worst, or do they also happen when you’re feeling emotionally okay?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / hairdresser_hairstylist_or_cosmetologist / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 3\nmanifestation: \"Before the depression really took over, there was a stretch of several weeks when I was still getting up and functioning more normally, but I was already hearing the voice and feeling sure someone at the salon was interfering with my schedule.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, it actually... it wasn't just when I felt this bad. That’s the weird part about all of this. Because before the depression really took over, you know, before I got to this point where I just want to sleep all day and feel like a dead battery... there was a stretch of several weeks where I was actually doing okay emotionally. I was still getting up every morning, functioning totally normally. I was doing my full hair and makeup routine, which takes like an hour because, you know, working in a salon, you have to look the part. If you show up looking a mess, clients are not going to trust you with their foils or their extensions. So I was doing all that, getting to work on time, drinking my iced coffee, chatting with the girls in the breakroom... I felt fine. 

But even during those weeks, I was already hearing the voice. I’d be back there by the shampoo bowls, feeling completely normal, and she would still be there, telling me I was ruining everything. And... well, there was something else happening then, too. During that same time when my mood was fine, I was absolutely sure that someone at the salon was interfering with my schedule. 

We use this booking software on an iPad at the front desk, and you can check it on your phone, and I just knew someone was going in and messing with my books. Like, I’d have a three-hour color correction blocked out, and suddenly a quick trim would be shoved into my processing time when I hadn't approved it, or my lunch break would just mysteriously vanish from the system. I was positive one of the other stylists or the receptionist was sabotaging me on purpose to make me look bad or make me run late for my clients. I was constantly checking my phone to see what they had changed next. 

So, yeah, to answer your question, it definitely wasn't just when my mood was at its worst. I was functioning fine, feeling emotionally okay, but I was already dealing with that cruel voice and knowing someone was messing with my appointments for weeks before this heavy, awful feeling really hit me.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.3, F06.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m Dr. ____. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, um, it's just... I've been having a really hard time at work lately, and it's starting to become a real problem. I'm a hairstylist, you know, and usually, I'm pretty quick on my feet, getting through my appointments, chatting with clients, the whole thing. But recently, it's like... I don't know how to explain it, but my hands feel like they’re moving through glue. It's the weirdest sensation. Like, even just doing the simple things I've done a million times, like sectioning someone's hair before a cut or, or mixing up the color in the bowl—which usually takes me like two seconds—it just takes me forever now. It's like my brain is telling my hands to move, but they're just dragging. And the worst part is, it's not just me feeling it in my head. People have started noticing. My manager pulled me aside the other day and asked if I was feeling okay because I'm just so much slower than I used to be, and I didn't know what to tell her because I don't really know what's going on myself, just that everything feels so heavy and takes so much effort now.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have a tremor in your hands when they’re at rest?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any shaking or tremors when I'm just sitting there. Like, if I'm just sitting in the breakroom on my phone, or watching TV at home, my hands are completely still. It's really just that heavy, dragging feeling when I actually try to use them. Like I said, it's just so bizarre because I'm used to being so fast. I mean, you have to be fast in a salon, especially on a Saturday when you have back-to-back clients and someone's processing while you're cutting someone else. You're constantly moving, constantly using your hands. And now, it's just this incredible effort to even lift the hairdryer. But no, no shaking at all when I'm resting them. Just the slowness. It's so frustrating because my mind knows what to do, but my body just won't cooperate, you know? It's like I'm moving underwater. But yeah, no tremors.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed or down most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I have. It’s actually... well, it’s most days, honestly. Like, I’ll wake up, and before I even get out of bed, there’s just this... this heavy, flat sadness sitting right in the middle of my chest. It’s not even like a sharp pain or crying sadness, it’s just this dull, heavy weight that makes it hard to even breathe sometimes. And it’s just there, all day. It makes me feel like... like there’s just no point in trying to build the life I was planning, you know? \n\nI had all these plans when I got my associate's and started at the salon. I was going to build up my clientele, maybe open my own suite eventually... but now, with this feeling, it just seems so impossible and pointless. Why even try when everything feels this bleak? I try to push through it, especially at work, because you have to put on a happy face for the clients. You can't just stand behind the chair looking miserable while you're doing a balayage. But it takes so much energy to fake it. By the time I get home, I’m just completely drained, and that heavy sadness is still right there waiting for me. It’s like a dark cloud that just follows me from the minute I open my eyes until I finally manage to fall asleep. It’s exhausting, honestly. Just... exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I really have. It’s like all the color has just drained out of everything. I used to love my job, I really did. I was always the one chatting up my clients, asking about their kids, their vacations, you know, just really connecting with them. I even used to stay late sometimes just to help the newer stylists practice their foiling techniques or color melting because I genuinely enjoyed it. I felt like I had this spark, this energy for it. And on my days off, I was always out doing something. I’d go hiking with my friends up at the state park—we’d pack a lunch, spend the whole day out there, just laughing and taking pictures. I loved being outdoors, breathing the fresh air. \n\nBut now... I don't know, it's just gone. That spark is completely gone. Now when I’m at work, I just keep my head down. I barely say a word to my clients unless I absolutely have to ask them about their hair. I don't want to talk, I don't want to engage. I just want to get through the cut and go hide in the breakroom. I avoid the other stylists, too. If I see them gathering to chat, I just walk the other way. And my friends... they keep texting me to go hiking or grab coffee, and I just make up excuses or ignore them. The thought of going out, of trying to pretend I care about a hike or a conversation... it just feels impossible. I don't get any joy out of it anymore. It’s like my battery is completely dead and I don't even care enough to try and recharge it. I just want to be left alone, which is so weird for me because I've always been such a people person, but now... I just don't have it in me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living or that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I mean... yeah. It’s hard to say out loud, but yes. I want to be really clear though, I don’t have a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. I’m not sitting at home looking at my shears or trying to figure out how to do something drastic. I don't think I could ever actually do that. But... I do get these thoughts, especially at night when I’m just lying there in the dark after a long shift. It just feels like everything is so incredibly heavy, and I catch myself thinking... I just think it would be so much easier if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. You know? Just drifted off and that was the end of it. \n\nBecause then I wouldn't have to wake up and face that horrible, flat feeling in my chest again, and... and honestly, nobody would have to deal with me anymore. Because I really feel like I’m just becoming this huge burden to everyone around me. Like my friends, the ones I mentioned who keep texting me to go out? I know they’re getting frustrated with me constantly flaking on them. I can tell from the tone of their messages, they're just over it. And at the salon, my manager is definitely noticing that I’m acting weird and not talking to my clients or the other girls. It’s just... it’s exhausting for me, but it has to be exhausting for them, too, dealing with someone who is just a total drag now. So yeah, I don't want to actively kill myself, but I just catch myself wishing that my body would just stop in my sleep. Just let me not wake up, so I don't have to keep forcing myself through every single day and dragging everyone else down with me. It just really feels like it would be a relief for everyone involved.\n\nDoctor: Have you started, stopped, or changed any medication or substance use recently?\n\nPatient: No... no, I haven't. I mean, I don't really take anything to begin with, honestly. Like, I'm not on any prescriptions or anything like that. The most I ever take is maybe some ibuprofen or Advil, you know, because standing behind the chair for eight or ten hours a day, your lower back and your feet just start screaming at you by the end of the shift. Especially when you're doing a long color correction or a full head of babylights where you're just stuck in one spot and you can't really sit down or take a break. But other than that, no. I don't take any daily pills or anything. \n\nAnd as far as, like, other stuff... I don't really drink or do drugs. I mean, back when I was in school getting my associate's, or even when I first started at the salon, the other stylists and I would sometimes go out for margaritas on a Friday night after we closed up, just to blow off some steam and complain about difficult clients or whatever. But I haven't done that in... I don't even know how long. Like I said earlier, I just avoid everyone now, so I definitely haven't been going out drinking. And I've never been into anything else, no smoking or whatever. So, yeah, nothing has changed. I'm not putting anything new into my body, and I haven't stopped taking anything because there was nothing to stop in the first place. It's just me and this heavy feeling, really. Just dealing with it on my own without taking anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a few days when you felt unusually energetic or overly confident, with much less need for sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. Unusually energetic? No, definitely not. Honestly, I can't even remember the last time I felt just normal energy, let alone extra energy. Like I said, it’s just this constant, heavy, dead-battery feeling all the time. I've never had a time where I didn't need to sleep. If anything, it's the total opposite right now. I just want to sleep all the time, even though I don't sleep well when I actually try. But no, I've never had a few days where I felt like I could conquer the world or anything like that. \n\nI mean, I used to be confident, sure. Like, when I first got my chair at the salon, I was so proud of myself. I felt like I really knew what I was doing, especially with color. I was always the one the manager would ask to double-check a toner formula or something because I just had a good eye for it. I felt really solid in my skills. But it wasn't, like, *overly* confident, you know? It was just normal confidence because I worked hard for my associate's and I practiced a lot. I never felt like I was invincible or better than everyone else or anything crazy like that. And I certainly never felt like I didn't need sleep. Sleep has always been really important to me, especially standing on my feet all day. If I don't get at least seven or eight hours, I'm just a wreck the next day at work. My hands get a little shaky when I'm trying to do precision cuts if I'm tired, so I've always prioritized sleep. So, no, I've never had anything like what you're describing. Never any bursts of crazy energy or not needing to sleep. Just the normal me, and now... whatever this version of me is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with, or treated for, any brain or neurological condition?\n\nPatient: No, never anything like that. I mean, my brain... well, besides all the weird stuff I've been telling you about with feeling so empty and the voices and everything... physically, my brain is fine. At least, I think it is. I've never had a doctor tell me otherwise. Like, I've never had a seizure or a stroke or anything scary like that. I don't have epilepsy. I don't even really get bad headaches, to be honest. Sometimes I get a little bit of a tension headache right at the base of my neck, but that's just from looking down at people's hair all day, you know? When you're constantly angling your head down to check a guideline or make sure a bob is perfectly straight, your neck just gets so stiff, and that can kind of travel up into your head. But that usually goes away if I just take a hot shower and kind of massage the back of my neck. \n\nI did hit my head once when I was little, maybe like seven or eight? I was at the park with my mom and I fell off the monkey bars and bumped my forehead on the woodchips. I got a pretty good goosebump, and my mom was so worried she took me to the pediatrician, but they just said to keep an eye on me and make sure I didn't throw up or fall asleep immediately. And I was totally fine, I was back to running around the next day. But that's literally the only time I can even think of where my head was involved in any kind of medical thing. I've never had an MRI or a CT scan or anything where they actually looked at my brain. So, as far as I know, neurologically, everything is completely normal. It's just... whatever is happening to me now, it doesn't feel like a physical brain problem, you know? It feels different. But yeah, no official diagnoses or treatments for anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don’t seem to hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yeah... actually, yes. It happens at the salon mostly. \n\nYou know how at the end of the day, or even just between clients if you have a minute, you have to do all this cleanup? Like sweeping up the hair around the chairs—which gets absolutely everywhere, by the way, it sticks to your shoes and your clothes—or taking all the dirty color bowls and brushes to the back sink to rinse them out before the color dries and stains the plastic? Well, sometimes when I’m doing that... like when I’m just quietly sweeping up or standing there rinsing out the bowls... I hear this voice. \n\nIt’s a woman’s voice. And she sounds like she’s right over by the shampoo stations. Our shampoo bowls are kind of tucked into this little alcove near the back of the floor, so it echoes just a tiny bit, but I can hear her so clearly. She just keeps saying that I’m ruining everything. Just... \"you're ruining everything.\" And her tone is so cruel. \n\nThe first time it happened, I actually dropped the broom and walked over to the bowls because I thought maybe one of the other stylists was back there on the phone having a fight with someone, or maybe a client had wandered back looking for the restroom and was upset. But there was nobody there. And it keeps happening even when the salon is completely empty. Like, if I’m the closing stylist for the night and I’ve already locked the front door, flipped the sign, and turned off the main lights, and it’s literally just me in the building finishing up... I’ll still hear her. Right near the shampoo stations. Just telling me I’m ruining everything. \n\nIt makes me freeze up, honestly. I just stand there holding a wet tint brush or whatever, and my heart starts pounding because I know for a fact I’m the only one in the building, or that all the other girls are up at the front desk, but the voice is just so real. It's right there. I haven't really *seen* anything, though. No weird shadows or people who aren't there, nothing like that. It's really just hearing her. And it's always when I'm trying to clean up, and she just won't stop telling me how I'm ruining it all.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices or other unusual experiences happen only when your mood is at its worst, or do they also happen when you’re feeling emotionally okay?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it actually... it wasn't just when I felt this bad. And I've never been into anything else, no smoking or whatever. So, yeah, nothing has changed. I'm not putting anything new into my body, and I haven't stopped taking anything because there was nothing to stop in the first place. It's just me and this heavy feeling, really. Just dealing with it on my own without taking anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a few days when you felt unusually energetic or overly confident, with much less need for sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I... I don't think so. Unusually energetic? No, definitely not. Honestly, I can't even remember the last time I felt just normal energy, let alone extra energy. Like I said, it’s just this constant, heavy, dead-battery feeling all the time. I've never had a time where I didn't need to sleep. If anything, it's the total opposite right now. I just want to sleep all the time, even though I don't sleep well when I actually try. But no, I've never had a few days where I felt like I could conquer the world or anything like that. \n\nI mean, I used to be confident, sure. Like, when I first got my chair at the salon, I was so proud of myself. I felt like I really knew what I was doing, especially with color. I was always the one the manager would ask to double-check a toner formula or something because I just had a good eye for it. I felt really solid in my skills. But it wasn't, like, *overly* confident, you know? It was just normal confidence because I worked hard for my associate's and I practiced a lot. I never felt like I was invincible or better than everyone else or anything crazy like that. And I certainly never felt like I didn't need sleep. Sleep has always been really important to me, especially standing on my feet all day. If I don't get at least seven or eight hours, I'm just a wreck the next day at work. My hands get a little shaky when I'm trying to do precision cuts if I'm tired, so I've always prioritized sleep. So, no, I've never had anything like what you're describing. Never any bursts of crazy energy or not needing to sleep. Just the normal me, and now... whatever this version of me is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with, or treated for, any brain or neurological condition?\n\nPatient: No, never anything like that. I mean, my brain... well, besides all the weird stuff I've been telling you about with feeling so empty and the voices and everything... physically, my brain is fine. At least, I think it is. I've never had a doctor tell me otherwise. Like, I've never had a seizure or a stroke or anything scary like that. I don't have epilepsy. I don't even really get bad headaches, to be honest. Sometimes I get a little bit of a tension headache right at the base of my neck, but that's just from looking down at people's hair all day, you know? When you're constantly angling your head down to check a guideline or make sure a bob is perfectly straight, your neck just gets so stiff, and that can kind of travel up into your head. But that usually goes away if I just take a hot shower and kind of massage the back of my neck. \n\nI did hit my head once when I was little, maybe like seven or eight? I was at the park with my mom and I fell off the monkey bars and bumped my forehead on the woodchips. I got a pretty good goosebump, and my mom was so worried she took me to the pediatrician, but they just said to keep an eye on me and make sure I didn't throw up or fall asleep immediately. And I was totally fine, I was back to running around the next day. But that's literally the only time I can even think of where my head was involved in any kind of medical thing. I've never had an MRI or a CT scan or anything where they actually looked at my brain. So, as far as I know, neurologically, everything is completely normal. It's just... whatever is happening to me now, it doesn't feel like a physical brain problem, you know? It feels different. But yeah, no official diagnoses or treatments for anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don’t seem to hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yeah... actually, yes. It’s... it’s kind of hard to talk about because it sounds so crazy, but yes. I actually mentioned voices a minute ago, and this is exactly what I meant. It happens at the salon mostly. \n\nYou know how at the end of the day, or even just between clients if you have a minute, you have to do all this cleanup? Like sweeping up the hair around the chairs—which gets absolutely everywhere, by the way, it sticks to your shoes and your clothes—or taking all the dirty color bowls and brushes to the back sink to rinse them out before the color dries and stains the plastic? Well, sometimes when I’m doing that... like when I’m just quietly sweeping up or standing there rinsing out the bowls... I hear this voice. \n\nIt’s a woman’s voice. And she sounds like she’s right over by the shampoo stations. Our shampoo bowls are kind of tucked into this little alcove near the back of the floor, so it echoes just a tiny bit, but I can hear her so clearly. She just keeps saying that I’m ruining everything. Just... \"you're ruining everything.\" And her tone is so cruel. \n\nThe first time it happened, I actually dropped the broom and walked over to the bowls because I thought maybe one of the other stylists was back there on the phone having a fight with someone, or maybe a client had wandered back looking for the restroom and was upset. But there was nobody there. And it keeps happening even when the salon is completely empty. Like, if I’m the closing stylist for the night and I’ve already locked the front door, flipped the sign, and turned off the main lights, and it’s literally just me in the building finishing up... I’ll still hear her. Right near the shampoo stations. Just telling me I’m ruining everything. \n\nIt makes me freeze up, honestly. I just stand there holding a wet tint brush or whatever, and my heart starts pounding because I know for a fact I’m the only one in the building, or that all the other girls are up at the front desk, but the voice is just so real. It's right there. I haven't really *seen* anything, though. No weird shadows or people who aren't there, nothing like that. It's really just hearing her. And it's always when I'm trying to clean up, and she just won't stop telling me how I'm ruining it all.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices or other unusual experiences happen only when your mood is at its worst, or do they also happen when you’re feeling emotionally okay?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it actually... it wasn't just when I felt this bad. That’s the weird part about all of this. Because before the depression really took over, you know, before I got to this point where I just want to sleep all day and feel like a dead battery... there was a stretch of several weeks where I was actually doing okay emotionally. I was still getting up every morning, functioning totally normally. I was doing my full hair and makeup routine, which takes like an hour because, you know, working in a salon, you have to look the part. If you show up looking a mess, clients are not going to trust you with their foils or their extensions. So I was doing all that, getting to work on time, drinking my iced coffee, chatting with the girls in the breakroom... I felt fine. \n\nBut even during those weeks, I was already hearing the voice. I’d be back there by the shampoo bowls, feeling completely normal, and she would still be there, telling me I was ruining everything. And... well, there was something else happening then, too. During that same time when my mood was fine, I was absolutely sure that someone at the salon was interfering with my schedule. \n\nWe use this booking software on an iPad at the front desk, and you can check it on your phone, and I just knew someone was going in and messing with my books. Like, I’d have a three-hour color correction blocked out, and suddenly a quick trim would be shoved into my processing time when I hadn't approved it, or my lunch break would just mysteriously vanish from the system. I was positive one of the other stylists or the receptionist was sabotaging me on purpose to make me look bad or make me run late for my clients. I was constantly checking my phone to see what they had changed next. \n\nSo, yeah, to answer your question, it definitely wasn't just when my mood was at its worst. I was functioning fine, feeling emotionally okay, but I was already dealing with that cruel voice and knowing someone was messing with my appointments for weeks before this heavy, awful feeling really hit me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, nothing like that. Honestly, I'm physically really healthy. I mean, my diet isn't exactly perfect, because when you work behind the chair, you really don't get proper lunch breaks. You just kind of shove a granola bar in your mouth or eat half a bagel in the breakroom while your client's toner is processing, or you just drink like three iced coffees to keep your energy up. So you'd think maybe my blood sugar would be all over the place with eating like that, but no, I've never had any issues with diabetes or anything even close to it. 

And my thyroid is totally fine, too. Actually, it's funny you mention thyroid problems, because in cosmetology school, they drill it into us that if a client comes in and their hair is suddenly thinning or getting super brittle and breaking off at the mid-shaft, we should gently suggest they go to their doctor and get their thyroid checked. It's like one of the main physical things that affects hair growth and texture. But my hair is super thick, and I actually had a full physical with a complete blood panel done right before I graduated and got my license. My mom insisted on it just to make sure I was good to go for working full-time, since being on your feet for ten hours a day is pretty demanding on your body. 

They checked literally everything—my iron levels, my thyroid, my blood sugar, all of it—and the doctor said my labs were absolutely perfect. I don't have asthma, I don't have high blood pressure, nothing. So, yeah, no major medical illnesses at all. Physically, my body is doing great, which is why it's just so frustrating that... well, that everything else feels so completely broken right now. But yeah, physically, I have a totally clean bill of health.
